Legal Operations and Compliance English

A legal operations and compliance English curriculum for contract workflow, policy implementation, audits, controls, investigations, privacy requests, training, third-party risk, and governance communication.

For legal operations staff, compliance managers, contract managers, policy owners, audit teams, investigation coordinators, privacy operations staff, and risk professionals.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify the agreement, owner, decision need, and document status before routing a contract request.
  • Translate a policy requirement into an assigned, evidenced process without confusing publication with operation.
  • Respond to an evidence request with the correct period, source, completeness, and limitations.
  • Attribute reported events, separate inference from observation, and route sensitive concerns for impartial review.
  • Clarify identity and requested actions, preserve receipt details, and route timing and disclosure decisions correctly.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Contract Intake and WorkflowUrgent is a signal, not a complete instruction+

Your goal

Clarify the agreement, owner, decision need, and document status before routing a contract request.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Urgent is a signal, not a complete instruction

Ben submits request L17 with the subject Urgent contract. It lacks an agreement type, business owner, and required decision date. Attached is an unsigned vendor service proposal for a two-month, $40,000 software pilot; an earlier nondisclosure agreement is also attached. Intake coordinator Mina must establish the transaction purpose, correct parties, required documents, personal-data involvement, and actual timing. Receipt is not legal approval. The business has not confirmed who can authorize spending or sign. Mina can coordinate triage but cannot decide contract terms or promise immediate signature.

Ben: Business requester / Mina: Legal intake coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which information is missing from intake?

Answer and explanation

D. Agreement type, business owner, and required decision date

The request lacks routing essentials even though a proposal and earlier nondisclosure agreement are attached.

2. What does the service proposal describe?

Answer and explanation

A. A two-month software pilot for $40,000

The attachment describes the proposed pilot, while signature and approval remain unconfirmed.

3. What can Mina do?

Answer and explanation

A. Coordinate triage and route the request

Mina's role concerns coordination, not the unconfirmed commercial or legal decision authorities.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The earlier NDA and the proposed services arrangement serve different purposes. Do not assume the NDA approves the pilot, spending, data processing, or signature. Reviewers need the relevant document package and actual parties, not an intake label alone.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
intake
Receiving and organizing information needed to handle a request.complete contract intake
matter
A legal or operational issue tracked as a defined item of work.open a matter
triage
Assessing a request to determine its route, priority, and next action.triage the request
business owner
The person accountable for the underlying business need.identify the business owner
requester
The person submitting a request, not necessarily its decision-maker.contact the requester
counterparty
The other party to the relevant agreement or transaction.verify the counterparty
legal entity
An organization or person recognized as capable of relevant legal rights and duties.confirm the legal entity
agreement type
The category of contract or document being requested.identify the agreement type
NDA
Nondisclosure agreement; terms governing specified confidential information.locate the NDA
MSA
Master services agreement; overarching terms for specified service arrangements.review the MSA
SOW
Statement of work; a document specifying particular work and related terms.reconcile the SOW
order form
A document setting out a particular purchase and its relevant terms.verify the order form
contract package
The related documents that together describe the proposed arrangement.assemble the contract package
unsigned proposal
Proposed terms that have not been signed in the supplied record.identify an unsigned proposal
signature authority
Permission to execute the relevant agreement on behalf of a party.verify signature authority
spend approval
Authorization to commit the relevant funds.obtain spend approval
decision deadline
The time by which a specified decision is needed.clarify the decision deadline
target signature date
A desired signing date whose commitment status must be clear.qualify the target signature date
review scope
The issues and documents included in an assessment.define the review scope
routing rule
A criterion determining where a request is sent.apply routing rules
CLM
Contract lifecycle management; processes and systems for managing contract stages.maintain the CLM record
version control
Management of document revisions and their status.maintain version control
turnaround estimate
An estimate of how long a defined response or task will take.qualify the turnaround estimate
legal approval
Approval within the defined legal review process, distinct from other authorizations.record legal approval

The required decision date and the target signature date may differ. Acknowledgment means the request was received. Triage, legal review, commercial approval, and execution have separate statuses and authorities; a system entry does not complete all of them.

C Notice the language

Clarification can be helpful under time pressure

A requester may hear questions as delay. Explain which missing facts let the team assign the right reviewer and distinguish a business decision deadline from a hoped-for signature. Acknowledge urgency without promising that required review can be skipped.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the request
I have received L17 and the attached proposal.
Explain the missing facts
To route it correctly, I need the agreement type, business owner, and decision date.
Clarify the transaction
What services will the two-month pilot actually include?
Verify the parties
Which legal entities would enter the agreement?
Separate the documents
The earlier NDA does not replace the service documents.
Identify the owner
Who is accountable for the business need?
Check authority
Who approves the spend, and who is authorized to sign?
Clarify urgency
What decision is needed by what date, and what happens if it is delayed?
Separate two dates
Is that the review deadline or the desired signature date?
Check data involvement
Will the pilot involve personal data or access to customer systems?
Keep status accurate
The proposal is unsigned and has not completed review.
Request the package
Please include the current proposal and all referenced terms.
Avoid a turnaround promise
We need the scope and reviewer availability before confirming timing.
Route specialist questions
I will flag the data-processing details for the appropriate reviewer.
Maintain one record
Keep the current version and status in the intake record.
Close the handoff
I will confirm the route, missing items, and next contact once triage is complete.
6 language and precision notes
To route it correctly
Explains why the question matters to the requester's progress.
Which entities
Distinguishes legal parties from brands or informal team names.
Does not replace
Separates the roles of related contract documents.
What decision
Makes urgency specific enough to prioritize responsibly.
Has not completed
States review status without declaring the proposal unacceptable.
Once triage is complete
Sets a process boundary without promising the substantive result.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which acknowledgment is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. L17 is received; routing information and required approvals remain outstanding.

Receipt and document presence do not establish review, spending approval, or execution authority.

2. Which question clarifies timing best?

Answer and explanation

B. What decision is needed by which date, and how does that differ from desired signature?

A specific decision and consequence distinguish the review need from signature, supplier pressure, and an unsupported duration estimate.

3. What should the reviewer receive?

Answer and explanation

A. The current proposal, referenced terms, party details, business purpose, and relevant data information

The complete relevant package supports review without substituting an incomplete or misleading description.

4. Which distinction is necessary?

Answer and explanation

A. Requester, business owner, spend approver, and signer may be different roles.

These roles and stages can differ, so authority must be established rather than inferred.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Urgent is a signal, not a complete instruction

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Business requester / Mina: Legal intake coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • decision deadline
  • NDA
  • review scope
  • version control
  • legal entity
  • business owner
  • triage
  • signature authority
  • intake
  • turnaround estimate
  1. Ben
    I marked L17 urgent. The supplier is waiting, and the business wants the software pilot moving this week.
  2. Mina
    To complete 1, I need the agreement type, business owner, and required decision date. Those details help us assign the right reviewer without unnecessary transfers.
  3. Ben
    The proposal is for a two-month pilot costing forty thousand dollars. I attached the confidentiality agreement we signed earlier as well.
  4. Mina
    Thank you. The 2 and the pilot services arrangement have different purposes. We need the current proposal and any referenced service terms, rather than treat the earlier confidentiality document as approval of the whole purchase.
  5. Ben
    I submitted it for Nell in operations. She owns the pilot objective, although I do not know who approves the final spend.
  6. Mina
    I will record Nell as the 3 and leave the spend approver unconfirmed. The person who requests work, the person who owns the need, and the person who can authorize it are not necessarily the same.
  7. Ben
    The slides say Orbit; the proposal has a longer company name. I nearly shortened it to match. Should I leave it as written?
  8. Mina
    We need the actual 4 for each side. Please preserve the name in the document and have the business confirm the intended parties, rather than replace a formal name with the brand from the slides.
  9. Ben
    Nell needs a decision about whether to proceed by the sixth of October at noon UTC. She hopes to sign by the ninth.
  10. Mina
    That gives us a specific 5 and a separate target signature date. I will record both and the business consequence of delay, but those dates do not establish reviewer availability or approval.
  11. Ben
    The pilot will use customer contact details. I am not sure whether the supplier only views them or also stores a copy.
  12. Mina
    Include that uncertainty in the 6. The data flow and access details may affect which specialists need to review. Please do not answer stored or not stored merely to make the form look complete.
  13. Ben
    There are two versions of the proposal in the email chain. The latest changes the support arrangement, but neither is signed.
  14. Mina
    Keep clear 7. Identify the current revision and preserve the relevant history so the reviewer sees the changed support terms rather than an outdated attachment.
  15. Ben
    Once the legal team reviews it, can I send it for signature, or should I wait for another approval?
  16. Mina
    We must verify 8 and the other required approvals. Legal review does not automatically approve the budget or make you the signer. The actual workflow will identify the necessary sequence.
  17. Ben
    The supplier keeps chasing. Could we say today, even though the reviewer is not confirmed?
  18. Mina
    I cannot give that 9 yet. I can acknowledge receipt and confirm that we are clarifying the route. Once the scope and reviewer availability are known, we can communicate a supported timing expectation.
  19. Ben
    I will get Nell's confirmation, the party details, the latest proposal, and the data-handling information into the same record.
  20. Mina
    Good. I will complete 10, identify the missing approvals, and confirm the next contact. We can respond to the urgency while keeping receipt, review, approval, and signature as separate statuses.

Answers and explanations
  1. intake Turn 2. Intake gathers the routing information; receiving an attachment does not establish legal or commercial approval.
  2. NDA Turn 4. The NDA concerns specified confidential information and does not automatically approve the separate pilot services arrangement.
  3. business owner Turn 6. Nell owns the business need, while spending and signature authority still require separate confirmation.
  4. legal entity Turn 8. The legal entity identifies the proposed contracting party; a brand name may not identify that party accurately.
  5. decision deadline Turn 10. The decision is needed on 6 October at noon UTC, separately from the desired 9 October signature.
  6. review scope Turn 12. The review scope must flag the unresolved data handling so an appropriate specialist can assess the actual arrangement.
  7. version control Turn 14. Version control identifies the current proposal and changes; neither unsigned revision should be presented as executed.
  8. signature authority Turn 16. Signature authority must be confirmed separately; legal review does not automatically authorize the requester to execute the agreement.
  9. turnaround estimate Turn 18. A turnaround estimate needs the relevant scope and reviewer capacity; supplier pressure alone does not justify today's completion promise.
  10. triage Turn 20. Triage determines the appropriate route, priority, and next action without deciding the contract's substantive legal or commercial terms.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: I marked L17 urgent. The supplier is waiting, and the business wants the software pilot moving this week.
  2. Mina: To complete intake, I need the agreement type, business owner, and required decision date. Those details help us assign the right reviewer without unnecessary transfers.
  3. Ben: The proposal is for a two-month pilot costing forty thousand dollars. I attached the confidentiality agreement we signed earlier as well.
  4. Mina: Thank you. The NDA and the pilot services arrangement have different purposes. We need the current proposal and any referenced service terms, rather than treat the earlier confidentiality document as approval of the whole purchase.
  5. Ben: I submitted it for Nell in operations. She owns the pilot objective, although I do not know who approves the final spend.
  6. Mina: I will record Nell as the business owner and leave the spend approver unconfirmed. The person who requests work, the person who owns the need, and the person who can authorize it are not necessarily the same.
  7. Ben: The slides say Orbit; the proposal has a longer company name. I nearly shortened it to match. Should I leave it as written?
  8. Mina: We need the actual legal entity for each side. Please preserve the name in the document and have the business confirm the intended parties, rather than replace a formal name with the brand from the slides.
  9. Ben: Nell needs a decision about whether to proceed by the sixth of October at noon UTC. She hopes to sign by the ninth.
  10. Mina: That gives us a specific decision deadline and a separate target signature date. I will record both and the business consequence of delay, but those dates do not establish reviewer availability or approval.
  11. Ben: The pilot will use customer contact details. I am not sure whether the supplier only views them or also stores a copy.
  12. Mina: Include that uncertainty in the review scope. The data flow and access details may affect which specialists need to review. Please do not answer stored or not stored merely to make the form look complete.
  13. Ben: There are two versions of the proposal in the email chain. The latest changes the support arrangement, but neither is signed.
  14. Mina: Keep clear version control. Identify the current revision and preserve the relevant history so the reviewer sees the changed support terms rather than an outdated attachment.
  15. Ben: Once the legal team reviews it, can I send it for signature, or should I wait for another approval?
  16. Mina: We must verify signature authority and the other required approvals. Legal review does not automatically approve the budget or make you the signer. The actual workflow will identify the necessary sequence.
  17. Ben: The supplier keeps chasing. Could we say today, even though the reviewer is not confirmed?
  18. Mina: I cannot give that turnaround estimate yet. I can acknowledge receipt and confirm that we are clarifying the route. Once the scope and reviewer availability are known, we can communicate a supported timing expectation.
  19. Ben: I will get Nell's confirmation, the party details, the latest proposal, and the data-handling information into the same record.
  20. Mina: Good. I will complete triage, identify the missing approvals, and confirm the next contact. We can respond to the urgency while keeping receipt, review, approval, and signature as separate statuses.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Urgent does not identify the decision

The lesson's contract intake says urgent but omits agreement type, business owner, and required decision date; this is fictional workflow practice, not legal advice.

  1. Legal operations coordinator: The contract intake says urgent. Could you clarify the agreement type before we identify the appropriate approval path?
  2. Business requester: I haven't included that or the business owner. I can see the request isn't ready for useful triage.
  3. Legal operations coordinator: Thank you. We also need the required decision date and why that timing matters.
  4. Business requester: Could you simply prioritize it first while I gather the missing information?
  5. Legal operations coordinator: We need enough context to assess priority through the actual intake process, rather than infer it from urgent.
  6. Business requester: Understood. Should I choose any familiar template to make the request more complete?
  7. Legal operations coordinator: Don't guess the template. First clarify the agreement and business need so the responsible team can route it.
  8. Business requester: I'll confirm the owner and required decision date, distinguishing a target from an external commitment.
  9. Legal operations coordinator: Good. Mark anything still unknown instead of filling the gaps with assumptions.
  10. Business requester: I'll return the clarified intake so the team can assess the request and its priority accurately.
A renewal or a new agreement?

Separate fictional case: a requester labels a vendor document renewal, but the intake does not identify the existing agreement or whether the proposed scope changed.

  1. Contract intake analyst: You labeled this a renewal. Could you identify the existing agreement and whether the proposed services have changed?
  2. Business owner: I haven't linked the existing agreement, and I need to check the scope against the new document.
  3. Contract intake analyst: Then we shouldn't route it solely on the assumption that it repeats the prior arrangement.
  4. Business owner: Would the same vendor name be enough to select the previous approval path?
  5. Contract intake analyst: Not by itself. The document type and changed scope may affect the appropriate triage questions.
  6. Business owner: I'll provide the existing agreement reference and identify the differences I can confirm.
  7. Contract intake analyst: Thank you. Please distinguish confirmed changes from points you want the reviewer to interpret.
  8. Business owner: Understood. I won't describe the new terms as approved just because we have used the vendor before.
  9. Contract intake analyst: Good. Once the context is clear, the responsible team can determine the applicable workflow.
  10. Business owner: I'll complete those details and keep the renewal label provisional until the document relationship is understood.
A signature deadline or a requested review date?

Separate fictional case: an intake form lists Friday without explaining whether it is a signature deadline, a meeting date, or a preferred review date.

  1. Legal intake specialist: The date field says Friday. Could you clarify what needs to happen by then?
  2. Commercial project lead: I meant our preferred review date, but the form doesn't explain that. It is not a confirmed signature deadline.
  3. Legal intake specialist: That distinction matters for triage. We should record the requested decision and timing accurately.
  4. Commercial project lead: Could you guarantee completion on Friday now that the date is only our preference?
  5. Legal intake specialist: No. The responsible team still needs to assess the request, scope, and capacity before confirming timing.
  6. Commercial project lead: I'll explain the meeting purpose and identify which business question we need help resolving.
  7. Legal intake specialist: Please also confirm the business owner and intended document version so the approval path isn't based on assumptions.
  8. Commercial project lead: Understood. I can supply those details without presenting the review request as a request to sign.
  9. Legal intake specialist: Good. We'll preserve Friday as your requested review date and await the team's actual response.
  10. Commercial project lead: I'll communicate that internally so nobody treats an intake date as a confirmed legal commitment.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Urgent is a signal, not a complete instruction
  1. Read Ben and Mina aloud, then swap roles. Separate the sixth-of-October decision from the hoped-for ninth-of-October signature.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. Reread the party-name check and the unresolved customer-data flow without inventing either answer.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify another urgent intake request

Request M22 attaches an unsigned services proposal. Dana owns the business need. A review decision is needed on 5 May; signature is desired on 9 May. The spend approver and authorized signer are not yet confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The document status is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unsigned

The proposal has not been executed in the supplied record.

2. Requester: "The business owner is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. Dana

Dana owns the need, which does not automatically establish every approval authority.

3. Coordinator: "The decision is needed on ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 5 May

The review decision date differs from the desired signature date of 9 May.

4. Requester: "The signer remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unconfirmed

No authorized signer has been identified, so the requester cannot assume that role.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Contract Intake and Workflow
Original fictional case: Ben submits request L17 with the subject Urgent contract. It lacks an agreement type, business owner, and required decision date. Attached is an unsigned vendor service proposal for a two-month, $40,000 software pilot; an earlier nondisclosure agreement is also attached. Intake coordinator Mina must establish the transaction purpose, correct parties, required documents, personal-data involvement, and actual timing. Receipt is not legal approval. The business has not confirmed who can authorize spending or sign. Mina can coordinate triage but cannot decide contract terms or promise immediate signature.
Language workshop: Urgent is a signal, not a complete instruction
Communication goal: Clarify the agreement, owner, decision need, and document status before routing a contract request.
Language guidance: The earlier NDA and the proposed services arrangement serve different purposes. Do not assume the NDA approves the pilot, spending, data processing, or signature. Reviewers need the relevant document package and actual parties, not an intake label alone.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I have received L17 and the attached proposal. / To route it correctly, I need the agreement type, business owner, and decision date. / What services will the two-month pilot actually include? / Which legal entities would enter the agreement? / The earlier NDA does not replace the service documents. / Who is accountable for the business need? / Who approves the spend, and who is authorized to sign? / What decision is needed by what date, and what happens if it is delayed? / Is that the review deadline or the desired signature date? / Will the pilot involve personal data or access to customer systems? / The proposal is unsigned and has not completed review. / Please include the current proposal and all referenced terms. / We need the scope and reviewer availability before confirming timing. / I will flag the data-processing details for the appropriate reviewer. / Keep the current version and status in the intake record. / I will confirm the route, missing items, and next contact once triage is complete.
Vocabulary: intake: Receiving and organizing information needed to handle a request. / matter: A legal or operational issue tracked as a defined item of work. / triage: Assessing a request to determine its route, priority, and next action. / business owner: The person accountable for the underlying business need. / requester: The person submitting a request, not necessarily its decision-maker. / counterparty: The other party to the relevant agreement or transaction. / legal entity: An organization or person recognized as capable of relevant legal rights and duties. / agreement type: The category of contract or document being requested. / NDA: Nondisclosure agreement; terms governing specified confidential information. / MSA: Master services agreement; overarching terms for specified service arrangements. / SOW: Statement of work; a document specifying particular work and related terms. / order form: A document setting out a particular purchase and its relevant terms. / contract package: The related documents that together describe the proposed arrangement. / unsigned proposal: Proposed terms that have not been signed in the supplied record. / signature authority: Permission to execute the relevant agreement on behalf of a party. / spend approval: Authorization to commit the relevant funds. / decision deadline: The time by which a specified decision is needed. / target signature date: A desired signing date whose commitment status must be clear. / review scope: The issues and documents included in an assessment. / routing rule: A criterion determining where a request is sent. / CLM: Contract lifecycle management; processes and systems for managing contract stages. / version control: Management of document revisions and their status. / turnaround estimate: An estimate of how long a defined response or task will take. / legal approval: Approval within the defined legal review process, distinct from other authorizations.

Reread the party-name check and the unresolved customer-data flow without inventing either answer. Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

REFERENCE
Course: Legal Operations and Compliance English
Professional audience: legal operations staff, compliance managers, contract managers, policy owners, audit teams, investigation coordinators, privacy operations staff, and risk professionals
Scope: All organizations, people, policies, thresholds, cases, and timelines are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not legal advice or a complete compliance program. Follow current applicable law, actual policies, retention and preservation duties, and qualified legal or privacy guidance. Source jurisdictions differ; no example creates a universal rule, deadline, privilege, or legal conclusion.
Lesson 01: Contract Intake and Workflow
Original fictional case: Ben submits request L17 with the subject Urgent contract. It lacks an agreement type, business owner, and required decision date. Attached is an unsigned vendor service proposal for a two-month, $40,000 software pilot; an earlier nondisclosure agreement is also attached. Intake coordinator Mina must establish the transaction purpose, correct parties, required documents, personal-data involvement, and actual timing. Receipt is not legal approval. The business has not confirmed who can authorize spending or sign. Mina can coordinate triage but cannot decide contract terms or promise immediate signature.
Language workshop: Urgent is a signal, not a complete instruction
Communication goal: Clarify the agreement, owner, decision need, and document status before routing a contract request.
Language guidance: The earlier NDA and the proposed services arrangement serve different purposes. Do not assume the NDA approves the pilot, spending, data processing, or signature. Reviewers need the relevant document package and actual parties, not an intake label alone.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I have received L17 and the attached proposal. / To route it correctly, I need the agreement type, business owner, and decision date. / What services will the two-month pilot actually include? / Which legal entities would enter the agreement? / The earlier NDA does not replace the service documents. / Who is accountable for the business need? / Who approves the spend, and who is authorized to sign? / What decision is needed by what date, and what happens if it is delayed? / Is that the review deadline or the desired signature date? / Will the pilot involve personal data or access to customer systems? / The proposal is unsigned and has not completed review. / Please include the current proposal and all referenced terms. / We need the scope and reviewer availability before confirming timing. / I will flag the data-processing details for the appropriate reviewer. / Keep the current version and status in the intake record. / I will confirm the route, missing items, and next contact once triage is complete.
Vocabulary: intake: Receiving and organizing information needed to handle a request. / matter: A legal or operational issue tracked as a defined item of work. / triage: Assessing a request to determine its route, priority, and next action. / business owner: The person accountable for the underlying business need. / requester: The person submitting a request, not necessarily its decision-maker. / counterparty: The other party to the relevant agreement or transaction. / legal entity: An organization or person recognized as capable of relevant legal rights and duties. / agreement type: The category of contract or document being requested. / NDA: Nondisclosure agreement; terms governing specified confidential information. / MSA: Master services agreement; overarching terms for specified service arrangements. / SOW: Statement of work; a document specifying particular work and related terms. / order form: A document setting out a particular purchase and its relevant terms. / contract package: The related documents that together describe the proposed arrangement. / unsigned proposal: Proposed terms that have not been signed in the supplied record. / signature authority: Permission to execute the relevant agreement on behalf of a party. / spend approval: Authorization to commit the relevant funds. / decision deadline: The time by which a specified decision is needed. / target signature date: A desired signing date whose commitment status must be clear. / review scope: The issues and documents included in an assessment. / routing rule: A criterion determining where a request is sent. / CLM: Contract lifecycle management; processes and systems for managing contract stages. / version control: Management of document revisions and their status. / turnaround estimate: An estimate of how long a defined response or task will take. / legal approval: Approval within the defined legal review process, distinct from other authorizations.
Speaking task: Read Ben and Mina aloud, then swap roles. Separate the sixth-of-October decision from the hoped-for ninth-of-October signature. Correct the ten gaps. Reread the party-name check and the unresolved customer-data flow without inventing either answer. Repeat the handoff with Nell as business owner and spending and signature authority still unconfirmed.
Partner: Ben: Business requester / Mina: Legal intake coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Request M22 attaches an unsigned services proposal. Dana owns the business need. A review decision is needed on 5 May; signature is desired on 9 May. The spend approver and authorized signer are not yet confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: The supplier keeps chasing. Could we say today, even though the reviewer is not confirmed?
Mina: I cannot give that turnaround estimate yet. I can acknowledge receipt and confirm that we are clarifying the route. Once the scope and reviewer availability are known, we can communicate a supported timing expectation.
Ben: I will get Nell's confirmation, the party details, the latest proposal, and the data-handling information into the same record.
Mina: Good. I will complete triage, identify the missing approvals, and confirm the next contact. We can respond to the urgency while keeping receipt, review, approval, and signature as separate statuses.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

02Policy Implementation and ControlsThe policy is published, but the check has no owner+

Your goal

Translate a policy requirement into an assigned, evidenced process without confusing publication with operation.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The policy is published, but the check has no owner

Fictional company policy H3 requires compliance preapproval before committing to client hospitality costing more than $100 per guest, including all charges. Other restrictions still apply at any amount. H3 was published on 1 September, but the intake check and evidence retention have no assigned operator. A proposed meal costs $960 for eight guests, with no commitment yet made. Policy owner Mara and expense operations lead Dinesh must define who checks the request, who decides, where evidence is kept, and how exceptions are escalated. The event has not been approved.

Mara: Compliance policy owner / Dinesh: Expense operations lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What triggers the stated H3 preapproval requirement?

Answer and explanation

A. More than $100 per guest before commitment

H3 uses a per-guest threshold above one hundred dollars and requires approval before commitment.

2. What is the proposed cost per guest?

Answer and explanation

B. $120

Nine hundred sixty divided by eight equals one hundred twenty dollars per guest.

3. What is missing from implementation?

Answer and explanation

B. An assigned check operator and evidence-retention arrangements

H3 has been published, but the operational ownership and evidence arrangements remain undefined.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The proposed meal is $960 divided by eight, or $120 per guest, so it exceeds the stated H3 threshold. That triggers review; it does not guarantee approval. Costs at or below $100 are not automatically acceptable because other restrictions still apply.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
policy
An organization's stated rule or principle for decisions and behavior.publish a policy
procedure
The defined steps for carrying out a task or requirement.implement a procedure
control
An action or arrangement designed to address a specified risk or objective.operate a control
control objective
The intended result of a control.define the control objective
policy owner
The role responsible for maintaining and interpreting a policy through the authorized process.identify the policy owner
control operator
The person performing the defined control activity.assign the control operator
approver
A person authorized to make the relevant approval decision.verify the approver
preventive control
A control intended to stop an unwanted event before it occurs.design a preventive control
detective control
A control intended to identify an event or condition after or as it occurs.operate a detective control
trigger
The condition that requires a defined action.specify the control trigger
threshold
A boundary used by a rule or decision criterion.apply the threshold
preapproval
Required approval before the specified action or commitment.obtain preapproval
commitment
An undertaking or obligation whose relevant meaning depends on the context.verify commitment status
control evidence
Records supporting whether and how a control was performed.retain control evidence
evidence repository
The approved location for maintaining supporting records.use the evidence repository
retention rule
A requirement governing how long and under what conditions records are kept.follow retention rules
exception
A departure from a defined requirement, subject to the relevant process.escalate an exception
override
An authorized or unauthorized bypass of a normal control, with status specified.review control overrides
segregation of duties
Allocation of incompatible responsibilities to different roles where required.assess segregation of duties
design effectiveness
Whether a control is suitably designed to address its objective.assess design effectiveness
operating effectiveness
Whether a control works as intended in practice over the relevant scope.test operating effectiveness
control frequency
How often or upon what events a control is performed.define control frequency
implementation gap
A difference between a requirement and its practical deployment.close the implementation gap
issue escalation
Raising an unresolved problem to the appropriate authority.document issue escalation

A document, a workflow design, and evidence of a completed check are different things. Publication confirms that H3 is available. Operating effectiveness requires evidence of how the relevant checks actually function, not only the existence of a policy file.

C Notice the language

Published does not mean embedded in daily work

A team may believe compliance is complete because a document has been distributed. Ask how a real request moves through the process. Distinguish the person maintaining the policy from the person operating the check and the person making the approval decision.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the gap
The policy is published, but the check has no assigned operator.
Name the objective
The control should prevent commitments that lack required preapproval.
Apply the correct basis
The threshold is per guest, including all charges.
Show the calculation
Nine hundred sixty divided by eight is one hundred twenty.
Separate trigger and decision
That amount requires review; it does not mean the event is approved.
Keep other rules visible
Below-threshold spending may still be subject to other restrictions.
Assign the activity
Who checks the request before a commitment is made?
Separate the decision
Who has authority to approve or reject the request?
Specify the evidence
Retain the request, calculation, decision, reviewer, and timing.
Check timing
The evidence must show that required approval preceded the commitment.
Choose the repository
Store the records in the approved location under the applicable retention rules.
Handle absence
Who covers the check when the usual operator is unavailable?
Avoid bypassing the rule
Do not split one event to disguise the amount.
Route uncertainty
Send an unclear case to the designated policy reviewer.
Test the process
Walk a real request through the steps and verify the retained evidence.
Close accurately
The design can be assigned now; actual operation still needs verification.
6 language and precision notes
Per guest
Makes the threshold basis explicit instead of substituting the total.
Requires review
Distinguishes the trigger from a favorable decision.
Before
Defines the sequence that makes this a preventive check.
Who covers
Makes continuity part of the operational design.
Actual operation
Focuses on performed work rather than the existence of documentation.
Still needs verification
Prevents a design decision from becoming an unsupported effectiveness claim.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which status statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. H3 is published, but the control is not yet fully assigned or evidenced.

Publication does not establish operational ownership or completed checks, and H3 uses a per-guest basis.

2. Which event statement matches the rule?

Answer and explanation

A. The $120-per-guest proposal requires preapproval before commitment.

The per-guest cost exceeds the stated threshold, and the absence of commitment preserves the required review sequence.

3. Which evidence is most useful?

Answer and explanation

A. The identified request, calculation, authorized decision, and timestamps showing the sequence

The linked records allow review of both the decision and whether it occurred before the relevant commitment.

4. What does a well-written procedure prove by itself?

Answer and explanation

D. The designed steps, not that they operated effectively in practice

Design documentation explains the intended process, while operation and effectiveness require separate evidence.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

Answers and explanations
  1. control operator Turn 2. The control operator performs the defined check; publishing H3 has not assigned that practical responsibility.
  2. preapproval Turn 4. Preapproval must occur before commitment under H3; an after-the-event receipt check does not fulfill that timing requirement.
  3. threshold Turn 6. H3 uses more than one hundred dollars per guest, not a thousand-dollar total-event threshold.
  4. approver Turn 8. The approver holds the relevant decision authority, which is distinct from checking intake completeness and arithmetic.
  5. control evidence Turn 10. Control evidence links the request, relevant calculation, authorized decision, reviewer, and timing to the actual event.
  6. evidence repository Turn 12. The evidence repository provides an authorized location for the linked records under applicable access and retention rules.
  7. issue escalation Turn 14. Issue escalation routes urgency to the designated alternative authority; it does not grant the requester permission to bypass the control.
  8. control trigger Turn 16. The stated trigger concerns one preapproval requirement; other restrictions can still apply below the amount threshold.
  9. design effectiveness Turn 18. Design effectiveness concerns whether the proposed control addresses its objective, not proof that it operated throughout past periods.
  10. Operating effectiveness Turn 20. Operating effectiveness requires evidence that the control actually worked as intended within the relevant scope and period.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A published policy is not an operating control

The lesson's policy is published, but the person performing the check and the person retaining evidence have not been identified.

  1. Compliance operations lead: The policy is published, but who performs the related check and who keeps its evidence remain unassigned.
  2. Business process manager: Isn't publishing the policy itself the control, since staff can now read what is expected?
  3. Compliance operations lead: A policy states an expectation. A control is an action or arrangement intended to help meet that expectation.
  4. Business process manager: Then publication doesn't establish that the related check actually happens or leaves evidence.
  5. Compliance operations lead: Correct. We need the process owner to define the action, responsibility, and appropriate record under our approved process.
  6. Business process manager: How does monitoring differ from performing the check in the first place?
  7. Compliance operations lead: Monitoring examines how the arrangement is operating; it doesn't substitute for an unassigned control action.
  8. Business process manager: I'll explain the distinction to the implementation team and identify the open ownership questions.
  9. Compliance operations lead: Good. Avoid describing the policy as implemented solely because the document is available.
  10. Business process manager: We'll confirm the control responsibilities and evidence arrangements before claiming that the operational change is complete.
A reminder is not evidence of a completed check

Separate fictional case: an approved internal process sends a reminder for a monthly access review; the reminder was sent, but no review result is recorded.

  1. Control coordinator: The monthly access-review reminder was sent, but no result from the review has been recorded.
  2. Process owner: Our tracker counted the reminder as completion. What distinction should we explain to the team?
  3. Control coordinator: The reminder prompts the action. Evidence of the review shows whether the required check was actually performed.
  4. Process owner: So an automated message isn't proof that someone assessed the access list or addressed any finding.
  5. Control coordinator: Exactly. The control record needs to reflect the actual action under this approved internal process.
  6. Process owner: Could monitoring the reminder delivery still be useful, even though it doesn't establish review completion?
  7. Control coordinator: Yes. It answers a narrower question about notification, not the effectiveness or performance of the access review.
  8. Process owner: I'll distinguish notification status from review status and ask the responsible reviewer for the actual result.
  9. Control coordinator: Good. Keep the missing evidence visible rather than treating the message timestamp as a completed control.
  10. Process owner: I'll update our explanation and confirm what work happened before changing the review's status.
A control design differs from an operating result

Separate fictional case: a team has approved a design for checking supplier bank-detail changes, but has not tested or implemented it; no real transaction instructions are discussed.

  1. Policy implementation lead: The design for the supplier-change check is approved, but it hasn't been implemented or tested.
  2. Process owner: Can we describe that as an effective control, or only as an approved design?
  3. Policy implementation lead: Only the design approval is established. Operating effectiveness concerns what happens when the control actually runs.
  4. Process owner: Then the policy expectation, control design, and operating evidence are three different layers of information.
  5. Policy implementation lead: Yes. We should explain them separately so approval of the plan isn't mistaken for proof of performance.
  6. Process owner: Would training attendance establish that the control is working once we introduce it?
  7. Policy implementation lead: Attendance shows participation in training, not successful operation. The actual process needs appropriate evidence and monitoring.
  8. Process owner: I'll keep implementation and testing pending in the status report and identify the responsible owners.
  9. Policy implementation lead: Good. We can discuss readiness without prescribing transaction steps or inventing an assurance finding.
  10. Process owner: I'll confirm the next implementation actions under our approved process before presenting any operating conclusion.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The policy is published, but the check has no owner
  1. Read Mara and Dinesh aloud, then swap roles. Say the $960 total, eight guests, and $120 per guest on the same basis.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. Distinguish the intake operator from the compliance approver and approval before commitment from checking a receipt afterward.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Apply another fictional preapproval rule

Policy J2 requires review before commitment for hospitality above $80 per guest, including all charges. A proposed event costs $540 for six guests. Other restrictions still apply. No approval or booking is recorded.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The proposed cost per guest is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. $90

Five hundred forty divided by six equals ninety dollars per guest.

2. Reviewer: "That exceeds the stated ___."

Answer and explanation

C. $80 threshold

Ninety is above eighty, so the stated preapproval trigger applies.

3. Coordinator: "Approval is required before ___."

Answer and explanation

D. commitment

The fictional rule defines a preventive sequence, not only an after-event review.

4. Reviewer: "The current approval status is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. not approved

No approval is recorded, and exceeding the threshold triggers review rather than automatic permission.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 02: Policy Implementation and Controls
Original fictional case: Fictional company policy H3 requires compliance preapproval before committing to client hospitality costing more than $100 per guest, including all charges. Other restrictions still apply at any amount. H3 was published on 1 September, but the intake check and evidence retention have no assigned operator. A proposed meal costs $960 for eight guests, with no commitment yet made. Policy owner Mara and expense operations lead Dinesh must define who checks the request, who decides, where evidence is kept, and how exceptions are escalated. The event has not been approved.
Language workshop: The policy is published, but the check has no owner
Communication goal: Translate a policy requirement into an assigned, evidenced process without confusing publication with operation.
Language guidance: The proposed meal is $960 divided by eight, or $120 per guest, so it exceeds the stated H3 threshold. That triggers review; it does not guarantee approval. Costs at or below $100 are not automatically acceptable because other restrictions still apply.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The policy is published, but the check has no assigned operator. / The control should prevent commitments that lack required preapproval. / The threshold is per guest, including all charges. / Nine hundred sixty divided by eight is one hundred twenty. / That amount requires review; it does not mean the event is approved. / Below-threshold spending may still be subject to other restrictions. / Who checks the request before a commitment is made? / Who has authority to approve or reject the request? / Retain the request, calculation, decision, reviewer, and timing. / The evidence must show that required approval preceded the commitment. / Store the records in the approved location under the applicable retention rules. / Who covers the check when the usual operator is unavailable? / Do not split one event to disguise the amount. / Send an unclear case to the designated policy reviewer. / Walk a real request through the steps and verify the retained evidence. / The design can be assigned now; actual operation still needs verification.
Vocabulary: policy: An organization's stated rule or principle for decisions and behavior. / procedure: The defined steps for carrying out a task or requirement. / control: An action or arrangement designed to address a specified risk or objective. / control objective: The intended result of a control. / policy owner: The role responsible for maintaining and interpreting a policy through the authorized process. / control operator: The person performing the defined control activity. / approver: A person authorized to make the relevant approval decision. / preventive control: A control intended to stop an unwanted event before it occurs. / detective control: A control intended to identify an event or condition after or as it occurs. / trigger: The condition that requires a defined action. / threshold: A boundary used by a rule or decision criterion. / preapproval: Required approval before the specified action or commitment. / commitment: An undertaking or obligation whose relevant meaning depends on the context. / control evidence: Records supporting whether and how a control was performed. / evidence repository: The approved location for maintaining supporting records. / retention rule: A requirement governing how long and under what conditions records are kept. / exception: A departure from a defined requirement, subject to the relevant process. / override: An authorized or unauthorized bypass of a normal control, with status specified. / segregation of duties: Allocation of incompatible responsibilities to different roles where required. / design effectiveness: Whether a control is suitably designed to address its objective. / operating effectiveness: Whether a control works as intended in practice over the relevant scope. / control frequency: How often or upon what events a control is performed. / implementation gap: A difference between a requirement and its practical deployment. / issue escalation: Raising an unresolved problem to the appropriate authority.
Speaking task: Read Mara and Dinesh aloud, then swap roles. Say the $960 total, eight guests, and $120 per guest on the same basis. Correct the ten gaps. Distinguish the intake operator from the compliance approver and approval before commitment from checking a receipt afterward. Repeat the repository and cover arrangements. Keep a completed policy publication separate from evidence of the control operating.
Partner: Mara: Compliance policy owner / Dinesh: Expense operations lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Policy J2 requires review before commitment for hospitality above $80 per guest, including all charges. A proposed event costs $540 for six guests. Other restrictions still apply. No approval or booking is recorded.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dinesh: Once we document these roles and test a sample request, can we say the control has always worked?
Mara: No. We can assess the design effectiveness of the defined process. A current walkthrough does not prove how earlier requests were handled or fill missing historical evidence.
Dinesh: Then we should separate the completed publication task from the operational work still needed, and assign the expense check explicitly.
Mara: Exactly. Operating effectiveness needs evidence from actual operation. Let us record the operator, approver, cover route, and repository, then verify that the next requests follow the intended sequence.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Compliance Audits and EvidenceA current screenshot does not answer a historical question+

Your goal

Respond to an evidence request with the correct period, source, completeness, and limitations.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A current screenshot does not answer a historical question

Auditor Talia requests evidence of monthly privileged-access reviews for January through March 2026. Record owner Hugo locates the January and February review packages but has not found March. A colleague suggests sending a screenshot taken on 2 April showing the current access-review setting. The screenshot does not establish whether the three monthly reviews occurred. Hugo must identify the records found, disclose the March evidence gap, and continue the authorized search. He must not backdate a new record or assert that the missing package proves the March review never happened.

Talia: Compliance auditor / Hugo: Control record owner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which period did Talia request?

Answer and explanation

D. January through March 2026

The request concerns three named historical months rather than the current configuration alone.

2. Which review packages have been found?

Answer and explanation

B. January and February

The brief states that two packages are located while March remains missing.

3. What does the April screenshot establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

D. The displayed current setting, not completion of all three historical reviews

A current setting image does not document whether the requested historical activities actually occurred.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Two located packages out of three requested months is a statement about evidence availability. It is not automatically a 66.7% control-effectiveness score. The March gap must be disclosed while the underlying activity and available alternative evidence are assessed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
audit scope
The subjects, boundaries, and period included in an audit.confirm the audit scope
evidence request
A request for records supporting a specified question or assessment.clarify the evidence request
PBC list
Prepared-by-client list; an auditor's requested information schedule in relevant practice.maintain the PBC list
review period
The time interval covered by an assessment.state the review period
population
The complete defined set of items relevant to a test.establish the population
sample
Selected items examined from a defined population.explain the sample
privileged access
Access with elevated permissions under the relevant system rules.review privileged access
review package
The linked records supporting a particular review.assemble the review package
source record
The originating record used as evidence.preserve source records
provenance
Information about the origin and history of an item.document evidence provenance
timestamp
A recorded date and time for an event.verify timestamps
metadata
Information describing a record, such as origin, dates, and version.retain relevant metadata
contemporaneous evidence
Evidence created at or near the event it records.locate contemporaneous evidence
retrospective account
A later description of an earlier event.label a retrospective account
completeness
Whether all required or relevant items are included on the stated basis.assess completeness
reliability
The extent to which information can be depended on for the intended purpose.assess evidence reliability
relevance
The connection between evidence and the question being assessed.assess evidence relevance
evidence gap
Missing information needed to support an assessment.disclose an evidence gap
exception log
A record of identified departures and their handling.reconcile the exception log
sign-off
A recorded approval or confirmation under the relevant process.verify reviewer sign-off
reperformance
Independently carrying out a procedure to check its result.document reperformance
redaction
Removal or obscuring of information for an authorized disclosure purpose.document necessary redactions
audit trail
A traceable record of actions, changes, and decisions.preserve the audit trail
management response
Management's factual reply and planned actions concerning an audit matter.prepare the management response

A later explanation may help an investigation, but it must be labeled with its actual creation date and source. Do not recreate a missing sign-off as if it were contemporaneous. Authorized redaction should protect information without disguising a material evidence limitation.

C Notice the language

An evidence gap needs a precise answer

Audit questions can feel accusatory when teams are busy. Treat the requested period and record type as shared criteria. Acknowledge what is missing without becoming defensive, inventing evidence, or making a broader conclusion than the records support.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Confirm the scope
You are requesting the monthly reviews for January through March 2026.
State what is available
We have located the January and February packages.
Disclose the gap
We have not yet located the March package.
Limit the conclusion
That gap does not by itself establish whether the review occurred.
Describe the screenshot
This image was taken on the second of April and shows the current setting.
Separate configuration from operation
It does not demonstrate completion of the three monthly reviews.
Identify the source
Each package will show its originating system, reviewer, and relevant dates.
Check completeness
Let us reconcile the requested months against the evidence index.
Preserve the record
We will retain the original files and relevant metadata.
Avoid backdating
Any later explanation will carry its actual creation date.
Handle sensitive content
Use approved access and redaction procedures without concealing relevant gaps.
Continue the search
We will check the authorized repositories and record the search result.
Clarify alternatives
Would these separately identified records help assess the missing period?
Keep the limitation visible
Alternative evidence must be described for what it actually proves.
Agree a follow-up
I will provide the search status and any additional records by the agreed date.
Close accurately
The submission covers two located packages and an explicit March evidence gap.
6 language and precision notes
Have located
Reports availability, not a conclusion about every control outcome.
Not yet located
Distinguishes a search gap from proof that an event did not occur.
By itself
Restricts the inference available from one fact.
Actual creation date
Prevents a later record from impersonating earlier evidence.
Separately identified
Keeps alternative evidence distinct from the missing original package.
Covers
Defines the scope of the submission rather than implying completeness.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which submission description is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. January and February packages are available; March remains an identified evidence gap.

The known record availability supports a bounded description, not historical completeness or a fabricated record.

2. Which question best tests the screenshot's relevance?

Answer and explanation

B. Does this current setting image show that the requested historical reviews actually occurred?

The image shows current configuration; it does not by itself establish historical configuration, completed reviews, or a complete quarterly evidence set.

3. How should a later employee explanation be handled?

Answer and explanation

C. Label its source and actual date and assess what it supports.

A retrospective account can be useful but must retain its identity and evidential limitations.

4. What does two packages out of three establish?

Answer and explanation

D. Current evidence availability for two requested months

Record availability alone does not calculate overall control effectiveness or establish the missing month's substantive result.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A current screenshot does not answer a historical question

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Talia: Compliance auditor / Hugo: Control record owner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • source record
  • evidence gap
  • review period
  • timestamp
  • audit trail
  • retrospective account
  • completeness
  • redactions
  • management response
  • review packages
  1. Talia
    The request covers monthly privileged-access reviews for January through March. Can you confirm which records your submission will include?
  2. Hugo
    We have located the January and February 1. I have not found March yet, so I will identify that gap rather than describe the quarter as fully documented.
  3. Talia
    I also have a screenshot marked review enabled. When was it taken? I do not want to mistake a current setting for a completed monthly check.
  4. Hugo
    The 2 is the second of April. It shows the setting visible then. It does not establish that each January-to-March review was performed or signed off.
  5. Talia
    That distinction matters. I need evidence of operation during the requested months, not only evidence that a review feature is currently enabled.
  6. Hugo
    Understood. The 3 remains January through March 2026. I will label the April image separately so it cannot be mistaken for a historical review record.
  7. Talia
    What is included in the two packages you have located? We need to understand how they connect to the control activity.
  8. Hugo
    Each should identify the 4, relevant user population, reviewer, date, decision, and exceptions. I will verify those contents rather than assume a file titled completed review contains every required element.
  9. Talia
    Do you have an index showing the requested months and the status of each item?
  10. Hugo
    I am reconciling 5 against that list. It will show two located packages and one missing package, with any further evidence added under its own source and date.
  11. Talia
    Please be careful not to turn the missing March package into an unsupported conclusion about whether the review occurred.
  12. Hugo
    I will call it an 6. It means we cannot presently support the activity with that package, not that we have established the review never happened.
  13. Talia
    If the reviewer remembers doing it, send that account too. Just keep its actual date visible; it cannot become the missing March sign-off.
  14. Hugo
    Any 7 will have its actual creation date and author. We will not recreate a sign-off and date it in March merely to make the index look complete.
  15. Talia
    Some records contain user details. Follow the approved sharing process, but make clear if any removal limits what I can assess.
  16. Hugo
    We will document necessary 8 and use the authorized access route. We should not conceal a relevant exception or missing field under a general statement that the package was cleaned up.
  17. Talia
    What will you do next to locate the missing material or determine what other evidence exists?
  18. Hugo
    I will check the authorized repositories and preserve the 9 of what we find. Any alternative records will be identified separately, with an explanation of what they show and what they do not.
  19. Talia
    Then send the two packages, the index, and the search status by the agreed follow-up date. Keep the March limitation visible.
  20. Hugo
    I will make that clear in the 10. The response will distinguish current configuration, the two located monthly reviews, and the outstanding March evidence question, without backdating records or inventing an effectiveness score.

Answers and explanations
  1. review packages Turn 2. The located packages cover two requested months; the March package has not yet been found.
  2. timestamp Turn 4. The screenshot was captured on 2 April, so its current configuration view is not evidence of all earlier review events.
  3. review period Turn 6. The review period is January through March 2026, which determines whether the submitted records answer the request.
  4. source record Turn 8. Source records connect the package to the originating activity and must be checked rather than inferred from a file title.
  5. completeness Turn 10. Completeness is checked against the defined requested items; two located files cannot silently stand for three months.
  6. evidence gap Turn 12. An evidence gap identifies missing support without by itself proving that the underlying March review did not occur.
  7. retrospective account Turn 14. A retrospective account is a later explanation and must retain its actual date and source rather than impersonate an original sign-off.
  8. redactions Turn 16. Authorized redactions protect information while preserving transparency about any effect on the evidence available for assessment.
  9. audit trail Turn 18. The audit trail records the search, sources, changes, and follow-up without altering the original evidence or its history.
  10. management response Turn 20. The management response should state the available evidence, unresolved gap, and next action rather than claim unsupported historical completion.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Talia: The request covers monthly privileged-access reviews for January through March. Can you confirm which records your submission will include?
  2. Hugo: We have located the January and February review packages. I have not found March yet, so I will identify that gap rather than describe the quarter as fully documented.
