06Third-Party Risk and Due DiligenceSimilar services do not necessarily create similar exposure+

Your goal

Compare vendor data flows and dependencies, identify missing evidence, and communicate unresolved risk accurately.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Similar services do not necessarily create similar exposure

Two vendors propose workforce reporting services. Vendor A proposes department-level counts; Vendor B requests names, work email addresses, and individual salary records. B also uses separate hosting and support subcontractors, but their locations and access are unconfirmed. Business lead Paolo prefers B because its demonstration looked easier to use. Risk coordinator Imani must clarify the actual service need, data necessity, subcontractor roles, controls, and contractual arrangements. Neither vendor is approved. A smaller stated dataset does not prove A is risk-free, and a completed questionnaire alone does not prove B is suitable.

Paolo: Business service owner / Imani: Third-party risk coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which proposed dataset is more detailed about individuals?

Answer and explanation

D. B's names, work emails, and salary records

B requests person-level identifiers and salary information, while A proposes counts at department level.

2. What is unknown about B's subcontractors?

Answer and explanation

C. Their locations and access

The brief identifies hosting and support subcontractors but leaves their locations and access unconfirmed.

3. What is the current approval status?

Answer and explanation

A. Neither vendor is approved.

The scenario explicitly leaves both approvals open pending relevant assessment.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Department-level counts may reduce some exposure relative to identified salary records, but the actual fields, group sizes, combinations, access, and use still matter. Do not label a dataset anonymous or a vendor risk-free merely because the proposal uses the word aggregate.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
due diligence
Appropriate investigation before or during a relationship to understand relevant facts and risks.conduct vendor due diligence
business rationale
The reason a service or relationship is needed.document the business rationale
data flow
How information is collected, moved, accessed, stored, and used.map the data flow
data necessity
Whether information is needed for the stated purpose.assess data necessity
purpose limitation
Restricting use to the relevant permitted purposes under applicable requirements.define purpose limitations
subcontractor
A party engaged by a supplier to perform part of the work.identify subcontractors
subprocessor
A further processor engaged in a processing arrangement under relevant data-protection rules.verify subprocessor roles
fourth party
A downstream provider on which a direct supplier relies.assess fourth-party dependencies
cross-border transfer
Movement or access to information across relevant national boundaries.assess cross-border transfers
onward disclosure
Further sharing beyond an initial recipient.identify onward disclosures
DPA
Data processing agreement or addendum governing specified personal-data processing arrangements.review the DPA
contractual safeguard
A contract provision intended to address a specified risk or obligation.assess contractual safeguards
security questionnaire
A structured set of questions about a supplier's security practices.evaluate questionnaire responses
assurance report
A report providing an assessment within a defined scope, period, and method.review assurance-report scope
audit right
A contractual or other right to examine specified records or practices.clarify audit rights
incident notification
Communication about specified incidents under the applicable rules or agreement.define incident-notification duties
retention period
The length of time information is kept under stated requirements.verify retention periods
deletion verification
Evidence supporting that specified information was deleted as required.request deletion verification
inherent risk
Risk considered before the specified controls or responses.assess inherent risk
residual risk
Risk remaining after the assessed controls or responses.describe residual risk
risk acceptance
An authorized decision to take a defined risk under the relevant framework.document risk acceptance
remediation
Work to correct an identified weakness or issue.track remediation
ongoing monitoring
Continuing review of relevant changes and performance after initial assessment.plan ongoing monitoring
exit plan
Arrangements for ending the relationship and handling dependencies and data.assess the exit plan

A questionnaire is one information source, not an approval. Assess the relevant evidence, scope, period, exceptions, and actual service arrangement. Downstream access and locations can affect the assessment; an unconfirmed detail should remain an explicit gap rather than become an assumed safeguard.

C Notice the language

Risk questions should connect to the service need

A business team may hear due diligence as opposition to its preferred supplier. Explain which exposure each question addresses and what evidence would help. Keep the usability benefit visible while distinguishing commercial preference from an authorized risk decision.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the benefit
The demonstration suggests B may be easier for the team to use.
Clarify the purpose
Which decisions does the reporting service need to support?
Compare the inputs
A proposes department counts; B requests person-level salary and contact data.
Challenge necessity
Which of those individual fields are needed for the agreed purpose?
Map the flow
Where is each field stored, accessed, and sent?
Identify downstream roles
What do the hosting and support subcontractors actually do?
Keep locations open
Their access locations are not yet confirmed.
Avoid a label shortcut
Calling the data aggregate does not by itself establish anonymity.
Assess evidence
The questionnaire is a starting point, not a completed approval.
Check scope
Does the assurance evidence cover the proposed service and current period?
Clarify safeguards
We need the relevant processing, access, retention, and notification arrangements.
Check the end of the relationship
What happens to the data and dependencies when the service ends?
Separate risk states
Assess the initial exposure and the risk remaining after verified controls.
Name the decision authority
Any risk acceptance belongs to the designated authorized role.
Monitor change
Material changes to data use or subcontractors need the agreed review.
Close the recommendation
Record the business benefit, evidence, unresolved gaps, and approval status together.
6 language and precision notes
Suggests
Recognizes the demonstration without treating it as complete production evidence.
Which fields
Links collection to the actual reporting purpose.
Not yet confirmed
Keeps an unknown location or access right visible.
By itself
Avoids assigning a legal or risk conclusion from one label.
Verified controls
Distinguishes claimed safeguards from assessed evidence.
Together
Prevents a favorable commercial summary from omitting unresolved risk.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is justified?

Answer and explanation

C. B proposes more person-level data and unresolved downstream access, requiring relevant assessment.

Actual data detail and dependencies affect the assessment without proving automatic approval or rejection.

2. What is a useful question about salary fields?

Answer and explanation

D. Are they necessary for the agreed reporting purpose, and who would access them?

The question connects collection and access to purpose rather than assuming every requested field is justified.

3. How should a questionnaire be treated?

Answer and explanation

C. As one source to evaluate alongside relevant supporting evidence and gaps

Questionnaire responses require contextual assessment and do not automatically verify controls or confer approval.

4. Who may accept an identified residual risk?

Answer and explanation

C. The designated authorized decision-maker under the actual framework

Risk acceptance requires defined authority and a recorded basis, not commercial enthusiasm or document receipt.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Similar services do not necessarily create similar exposure

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Paolo: Business service owner / Imani: Third-party risk coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • business rationale
  • subcontractor
  • contractual safeguards
  • data flow
  • risk acceptance
  • cross-border transfers
  • data necessity
  • security questionnaire
  • inherent risk
  • ongoing monitoring
  1. Paolo
    B's demonstration was easier to use. Both vendors produce workforce reports, so I hoped the risk review could be the same for each.
  2. Imani
    Let us preserve that usability benefit while checking the 1. Which decisions do the reports need to support, and what information is actually necessary to make them?
  3. Paolo
    We need department-level planning. A proposes counts by department, while B asks for employee names, work emails, and individual salaries.
  4. Imani
    That makes 2 an important question. We should ask why each person-level field is needed instead of assuming a more detailed input always produces a more suitable service.
  5. Paolo
    I just found two other companies in B's diagram: hosting and support. The arrows do not show whether support staff can read the salary records.
  6. Imani
    We need the 3 and access roles. A diagram should show where the information is stored, who can reach it, and what each downstream provider does with it.
  7. Paolo
    The sales team called them standard subcontractors. Does that description settle the question?
  8. Imani
    No. We need to identify each 4 and its actual role. Whether a particular party is a subprocessor under the relevant arrangement needs assessment, not an assumption from a sales label.
  9. Paolo
    We have not been told where the hosting and support activities occur. Should I put our own country in the form for now?
  10. Imani
    Leave the location unconfirmed and flag possible 5 for the appropriate review. An assumed location can distort both the risk assessment and the contractual questions that follow.
  11. Paolo
    A's proposal is simpler. Can we mark it low risk without asking anything further because it only mentions department counts?
  12. Imani
    Not automatically. Its 6 depends on the actual fields, group sizes, combinations, access, and use. Counts can reduce some exposure, but the label aggregate does not establish anonymity or remove every service risk.
  13. Paolo
    B returned the questionnaire this morning. Purchasing is asking whether completed means cleared. What should I tell them is still outstanding?
  14. Imani
    The 7 is one source of information. We still need relevant evidence, its scope and date, any exceptions, and the actual arrangements for this service.
  15. Paolo
    What should the agreement and operational plan cover beyond the questionnaire answers?
  16. Imani
    The appropriate 8 and operating arrangements need review: permitted use, access, downstream providers, retention, incident communication, and what happens at exit. A document title alone does not settle those questions.
  17. Paolo
    If some uncertainty remains after those checks, can the business record it and decide whether to proceed?
  18. Imani
    Any 9 must follow the actual authority framework. The decision-maker needs the remaining exposure and evidence gaps, not a summary that hides them behind a favorable demonstration.
  19. Paolo
    I will request the data-flow details and field rationale, and keep both vendors unapproved until the relevant decision is made.
  20. Imani
    Good. Include 10 in the proposal as well. Data use and subcontractors can change after onboarding, so the relationship needs a defined review process rather than a one-time green label.

Answers and explanations
  1. business rationale Turn 2. The business rationale defines the needed reporting outcome, which helps assess whether each vendor's proposed inputs are justified.
  2. data necessity Turn 4. Data necessity asks whether each individual field is required for the stated department-planning purpose rather than merely requested by the vendor.
  3. data flow Turn 6. The data flow should identify storage, access, movement, and downstream recipients, including the unconfirmed support access.
  4. subcontractor Turn 8. A subcontractor label does not establish actual activities, access, locations, or the appropriate data-processing role.
  5. cross-border transfers Turn 10. Unconfirmed storage or access locations require assessment; staff must not insert an assumed country to complete a form.
  6. inherent risk Turn 12. Inherent risk still needs assessment of actual data, context, access, and use; department counts do not establish zero exposure.
  7. security questionnaire Turn 14. The questionnaire is a source of vendor claims to assess, not independent proof of every control or an approval decision.
  8. contractual safeguards Turn 16. Contractual safeguards address defined obligations, but their adequacy depends on the actual service, data roles, and applicable requirements.
  9. risk acceptance Turn 18. Risk acceptance is an authorized decision about defined remaining exposure, not permission created by an incomplete intake form.
  10. ongoing monitoring Turn 20. Ongoing monitoring checks material changes after initial assessment, including changes in data use and downstream dependencies.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Paolo: B's demonstration was easier to use. Both vendors produce workforce reports, so I hoped the risk review could be the same for each.
  2. Imani: Let us preserve that usability benefit while checking the business rationale. Which decisions do the reports need to support, and what information is actually necessary to make them?
  3. Paolo: We need department-level planning. A proposes counts by department, while B asks for employee names, work emails, and individual salaries.
  4. Imani: That makes data necessity an important question. We should ask why each person-level field is needed instead of assuming a more detailed input always produces a more suitable service.
  5. Paolo: I just found two other companies in B's diagram: hosting and support. The arrows do not show whether support staff can read the salary records.
  6. Imani: We need the data flow and access roles. A diagram should show where the information is stored, who can reach it, and what each downstream provider does with it.

  8. Imani: No. We need to identify each subcontractor and its actual role. Whether a particular party is a subprocessor under the relevant arrangement needs assessment, not an assumption from a sales label.
  9. Paolo: We have not been told where the hosting and support activities occur. Should I put our own country in the form for now?
  10. Imani: Leave the location unconfirmed and flag possible cross-border transfers for the appropriate review. An assumed location can distort both the risk assessment and the contractual questions that follow.
  11. Paolo: A's proposal is simpler. Can we mark it low risk without asking anything further because it only mentions department counts?
  12. Imani: Not automatically. Its inherent risk depends on the actual fields, group sizes, combinations, access, and use. Counts can reduce some exposure, but the label aggregate does not establish anonymity or remove every service risk.
  13. Paolo: B returned the questionnaire this morning. Purchasing is asking whether completed means cleared. What should I tell them is still outstanding?

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Similar services, different data-processing scope

The lesson's vendors provide similar services, but one processes more personal data and uses additional subcontractors.

  1. Procurement risk lead: The vendors offer similar services, but one handles more personal data and relies on additional subcontractors.
  2. Business sponsor: Can we treat their third-party risk as equivalent because the service descriptions look alike?
  3. Procurement risk lead: No. The data-processing scope and subcontractor arrangements need comparison alongside the business functionality.
  4. Business sponsor: Does handling more data automatically make that vendor unacceptable, or is further due diligence needed?
  5. Procurement risk lead: Further assessment is needed. We shouldn't convert a scope difference into an automatic risk decision.
  6. Business sponsor: Then compare both vendors against the same criteria and make the differences and unknowns visible.
  7. Procurement risk lead: Agreed. The vendor questionnaire can support the review, but responses need appropriate evidence and assessment.
  8. Business sponsor: I want the decision to reflect the tradeoff, including any operational benefit of the broader processing.
  9. Procurement risk lead: We'll identify that benefit only where supported, and keep approval with the authorized reviewers.
  10. Business sponsor: Good. Bring a consistent comparison rather than a recommendation based solely on price or the questionnaire's completion.
A completed questionnaire versus supporting records

Separate fictional case: vendor A returns a complete questionnaire without attachments; vendor B returns fewer answers with supporting records. Overall assurance has not been assessed.

  1. Vendor risk analyst: A returned every questionnaire answer without attachments; B supplied fewer answers but included supporting records.
  2. Procurement manager: Does the complete questionnaire make A the lower-risk option in our comparison?
  3. Vendor risk analyst: Not automatically. Completion and evidence quality are different criteria, and both vendors still need assessment.
  4. Procurement manager: Then B's attachments don't prove it is stronger overall either, since some answers remain missing.
  5. Vendor risk analyst: Correct. We should compare the same questions, evidence expectations, and unresolved gaps for each vendor.
  6. Procurement manager: Could you distinguish self-reported statements from the supporting material that has actually been reviewed?
  7. Vendor risk analyst: Yes. Due diligence should show the basis for each conclusion rather than count pages or completed fields.
  8. Procurement manager: The tradeoff is a fuller response from A versus more documentation from B, with overall adequacy still open.
  9. Vendor risk analyst: Exactly. I'll request the relevant missing information through the approved process before recommending an assessment outcome.
  10. Procurement manager: I'll keep both statuses pending and avoid labeling questionnaire completion as vendor approval.
A narrow data pilot or the full integration

Separate fictional case: a vendor proposes a narrow pilot using synthetic records or a full integration using customer records; functionality and risk reviews for both are incomplete.

  1. Business project lead: We can explore a synthetic-data pilot or a full integration using customer records. How should we compare them?
  2. Third-party risk reviewer: Use the same business question while distinguishing data-processing scope, functionality tested, and review requirements.
  3. Business project lead: The narrow pilot limits real-data exposure, but it may not test every production requirement.
  4. Third-party risk reviewer: Correct. That is a useful tradeoff, not proof that either option is approved or sufficient.
  5. Business project lead: Could a successful synthetic test establish that the full integration has passed due diligence?
  6. Third-party risk reviewer: No. It would provide evidence within that test scope, leaving the broader processing and operational questions open.
  7. Business project lead: Then the vendor questionnaire should distinguish the proposed pilot from the later integration rather than merge them.
  8. Third-party risk reviewer: Exactly. We need the actual scope and relevant evidence for whichever arrangement is considered.
  9. Business project lead: I'll state the narrower test's limits and ask which approvals are needed before either route proceeds.
  10. Third-party risk reviewer: I'll coordinate that assessment without turning reduced scope into a blanket assurance claim.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Similar services do not necessarily create similar exposure
  1. Read Paolo and Imani aloud, then swap roles. Compare department counts with named salary records without assuming either supplier is approved.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. Ask about each downstream provider, its access, and location while leaving unknown details unconfirmed.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Compare another pair of proposed services

Vendor C proposes site-level totals. Vendor D requests named staff records and uses an external support provider whose access is unknown. Neither is approved, and the business purpose needs clarification.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The unresolved support issue is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. access

The support provider's ability to reach the records has not been established.

2. Owner: "We must clarify the service's ___."

Answer and explanation

D. business purpose

The required purpose helps determine which inputs and service features are actually necessary.

3. Coordinator: "Neither supplier is currently ___."

Answer and explanation

C. approved

The brief leaves both vendor decisions open rather than favoring one automatically.

4. Owner: "A site-level-total label does not prove ___."

Answer and explanation

C. zero risk

Data context, combinations, access, and other service risks still require assessment.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Lesson 06: Third-Party Risk and Due Diligence
Original fictional case: Two vendors propose workforce reporting services. Vendor A proposes department-level counts; Vendor B requests names, work email addresses, and individual salary records. B also uses separate hosting and support subcontractors, but their locations and access are unconfirmed. Business lead Paolo prefers B because its demonstration looked easier to use. Risk coordinator Imani must clarify the actual service need, data necessity, subcontractor roles, controls, and contractual arrangements. Neither vendor is approved. A smaller stated dataset does not prove A is risk-free, and a completed questionnaire alone does not prove B is suitable.
Language workshop: Similar services do not necessarily create similar exposure
Communication goal: Compare vendor data flows and dependencies, identify missing evidence, and communicate unresolved risk accurately.
Language guidance: Department-level counts may reduce some exposure relative to identified salary records, but the actual fields, group sizes, combinations, access, and use still matter. Do not label a dataset anonymous or a vendor risk-free merely because the proposal uses the word aggregate.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The demonstration suggests B may be easier for the team to use. / Which decisions does the reporting service need to support? / A proposes department counts; B requests person-level salary and contact data. / Which of those individual fields are needed for the agreed purpose? / Where is each field stored, accessed, and sent? / What do the hosting and support subcontractors actually do? / Their access locations are not yet confirmed. / Calling the data aggregate does not by itself establish anonymity. / The questionnaire is a starting point, not a completed approval. / Does the assurance evidence cover the proposed service and current period? / We need the relevant processing, access, retention, and notification arrangements. / What happens to the data and dependencies when the service ends? / Assess the initial exposure and the risk remaining after verified controls. / Any risk acceptance belongs to the designated authorized role. / Material changes to data use or subcontractors need the agreed review. / Record the business benefit, evidence, unresolved gaps, and approval status together.
Vocabulary: due diligence: Appropriate investigation before or during a relationship to understand relevant facts and risks. / business rationale: The reason a service or relationship is needed. / data flow: How information is collected, moved, accessed, stored, and used. / data necessity: Whether information is needed for the stated purpose. / purpose limitation: Restricting use to the relevant permitted purposes under applicable requirements. / subcontractor: A party engaged by a supplier to perform part of the work. / subprocessor: A further processor engaged in a processing arrangement under relevant data-protection rules. / fourth party: A downstream provider on which a direct supplier relies. / cross-border transfer: Movement or access to information across relevant national boundaries. / onward disclosure: Further sharing beyond an initial recipient. / DPA: Data processing agreement or addendum governing specified personal-data processing arrangements. / contractual safeguard: A contract provision intended to address a specified risk or obligation. / security questionnaire: A structured set of questions about a supplier's security practices. / assurance report: A report providing an assessment within a defined scope, period, and method. / audit right: A contractual or other right to examine specified records or practices. / incident notification: Communication about specified incidents under the applicable rules or agreement. / retention period: The length of time information is kept under stated requirements. / deletion verification: Evidence supporting that specified information was deleted as required. / inherent risk: Risk considered before the specified controls or responses. / residual risk: Risk remaining after the assessed controls or responses. / risk acceptance: An authorized decision to take a defined risk under the relevant framework. / remediation: Work to correct an identified weakness or issue. / ongoing monitoring: Continuing review of relevant changes and performance after initial assessment. / exit plan: Arrangements for ending the relationship and handling dependencies and data.
Speaking task: Read Paolo and Imani aloud, then swap roles. Compare department counts with named salary records without assuming either supplier is approved. Correct the ten gaps. Ask about each downstream provider, its access, and location while leaving unknown details unconfirmed. Repeat the recommendation with the usability benefit, evidence gaps, decision authority, and continuing review arrangements all visible.
Partner: Paolo: Business service owner / Imani: Third-party risk coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Vendor C proposes site-level totals. Vendor D requests named staff records and uses an external support provider whose access is unknown. Neither is approved, and the business purpose needs clarification.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Paolo: If some uncertainty remains after those checks, can the business record it and decide whether to proceed?
Imani: Any risk acceptance must follow the actual authority framework. The decision-maker needs the remaining exposure and evidence gaps, not a summary that hides them behind a favorable demonstration.
Paolo: I will request the data-flow details and field rationale, and keep both vendors unapproved until the relevant decision is made.
Imani: Good. Include ongoing monitoring in the proposal as well. Data use and subcontractors can change after onboarding, so the relationship needs a defined review process rather than a one-time green label.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Legal Operations and Compliance English
Professional audience: legal operations staff, compliance managers, contract managers, policy owners, audit teams, investigation coordinators, privacy operations staff, and risk professionals
Scope: All organizations, people, policies, thresholds, cases, and timelines are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not legal advice or a complete compliance program. Follow current applicable law, actual policies, retention and preservation duties, and qualified legal or privacy guidance. Lesson 06: Third-Party Risk and Due Diligence
Original fictional case: Two vendors propose workforce reporting services. Vendor A proposes department-level counts; Vendor B requests names, work email addresses, and individual salary records. B also uses separate hosting and support subcontractors, but their locations and access are unconfirmed. Business lead Paolo prefers B because its demonstration looked easier to use. Risk coordinator Imani must clarify the actual service need, data necessity, subcontractor roles, controls, and contractual arrangements. Neither vendor is approved. A smaller stated dataset does not prove A is risk-free, and a completed questionnaire alone does not prove B is suitable.
Language workshop: Similar services do not necessarily create similar exposure
Communication goal: Compare vendor data flows and dependencies, identify missing evidence, and communicate unresolved risk accurately.
Language guidance: Department-level counts may reduce some exposure relative to identified salary records, but the actual fields, group sizes, combinations, access, and use still matter. Do not label a dataset anonymous or a vendor risk-free merely because the proposal uses the word aggregate.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The demonstration suggests B may be easier for the team to use. / Which decisions does the reporting service need to support? / A proposes department counts; B requests person-level salary and contact data. / Which of those individual fields are needed for the agreed purpose? / Where is each field stored, accessed, and sent? / What do the hosting and support subcontractors actually do? / Their access locations are not yet confirmed. / Calling the data aggregate does not by itself establish anonymity. / The questionnaire is a starting point, not a completed approval. / Does the assurance evidence cover the proposed service and current period? / We need the relevant processing, access, retention, and notification arrangements. / What happens to the data and dependencies when the service ends? / Assess the initial exposure and the risk remaining after verified controls. / Any risk acceptance belongs to the designated authorized role. / Material changes to data use or subcontractors need the agreed review. / Record the business benefit, evidence, unresolved gaps, and approval status together.
Vocabulary: due diligence: Appropriate investigation before or during a relationship to understand relevant facts and risks. / business rationale: The reason a service or relationship is needed. / data flow: How information is collected, moved, accessed, stored, and used. / data necessity: Whether information is needed for the stated purpose. / purpose limitation: Restricting use to the relevant permitted purposes under applicable requirements. / subcontractor: A party engaged by a supplier to perform part of the work. / subprocessor: A further processor engaged in a processing arrangement under relevant data-protection rules. / fourth party: A downstream provider on which a direct supplier relies. / cross-border transfer: Movement or access to information across relevant national boundaries. / onward disclosure: Further sharing beyond an initial recipient. / DPA: Data processing agreement or addendum governing specified personal-data processing arrangements. / contractual safeguard: A contract provision intended to address a specified risk or obligation. / security questionnaire: A structured set of questions about a supplier's security practices. / assurance report: A report providing an assessment within a defined scope, period, and method. / audit right: A contractual or other right to examine specified records or practices. / incident notification: Communication about specified incidents under the applicable rules or agreement. / retention period: The length of time information is kept under stated requirements. / deletion verification: Evidence supporting that specified information was deleted as required. / inherent risk: Risk considered before the specified controls or responses. / residual risk: Risk remaining after the assessed controls or responses. / risk acceptance: An authorized decision to take a defined risk under the relevant framework. / remediation: Work to correct an identified weakness or issue. / ongoing monitoring: Continuing review of relevant changes and performance after initial assessment. / exit plan: Arrangements for ending the relationship and handling dependencies and data.
Speaking task: Read Paolo and Imani aloud, then swap roles. Compare department counts with named salary records without assuming either supplier is approved. Correct the ten gaps. Ask about each downstream provider, its access, and location while leaving unknown details unconfirmed. Repeat the recommendation with the usability benefit, evidence gaps, decision authority, and continuing review arrangements all visible.
Partner: Paolo: Business service owner / Imani: Third-party risk coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Vendor C proposes site-level totals. Vendor D requests named staff records and uses an external support provider whose access is unknown. Neither is approved, and the business purpose needs clarification.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Paolo: If some uncertainty remains after those checks, can the business record it and decide whether to proceed?
Imani: Any risk acceptance must follow the actual authority framework. The decision-maker needs the remaining exposure and evidence gaps, not a summary that hides them behind a favorable demonstration.
Paolo: I will request the data-flow details and field rationale, and keep both vendors unapproved until the relevant decision is made.
Imani: Good. Include ongoing monitoring in the proposal as well. Data use and subcontractors can change after onboarding, so the relationship needs a defined review process rather than a one-time green label.
END REFERENCE
07Training, Attestations, and CultureCompletion is not the same as understanding+

Your goal

Report completion separately from understanding and explain a policy through precise, role-relevant examples.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Completion is not the same as understanding

The learning system shows 490 completions among 500 assigned employees, or 98%. Separately, 20 volunteers answer an application question; only eight answer correctly. Fictional policy W4 requires disclosure of outside paid work before it begins, including weekend work. Disclosure is not automatic prohibition, and the review outcome depends on the actual policy process. Employees still ask whether weekend assignments count. Training coordinator Sofia and compliance adviser Ren must report the two measures accurately and improve the explanation. The volunteer group is not established as representative of all 500 employees.

Sofia: Compliance training coordinator / Ren: Compliance adviser

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the 98% figure measure?

Answer and explanation

B. Recorded course completion among 500 assigned employees

Four hundred ninety divided by five hundred measures completion, not every aspect of knowledge or conduct.

2. How many volunteers answered correctly?

Answer and explanation

C. Eight of twenty

The application check reports eight correct answers in the separate twenty-person volunteer group.

3. What does W4 require for paid weekend work?

Answer and explanation

A. Disclosure before it begins

The fictional rule explicitly includes weekend work and requires prior disclosure without establishing automatic rejection.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Completion is 490 divided by 500, or 98%, leaving 10 without recorded completion. The volunteer application result is eight divided by 20, or 40%. Different populations and measures prevent either percentage from replacing the other.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
LMS
Learning management system; a platform for assigning, delivering, and recording training.review LMS records
completion rate
The share of an assigned population meeting a defined completion condition.calculate the completion rate
assigned cohort
The defined group required to complete a learning activity.verify the assigned cohort
completion criterion
The condition used to mark an activity complete.define completion criteria
attestation
A recorded statement confirming specified facts or understanding, with its meaning defined.obtain an attestation
acknowledgment
Confirmation of receipt or awareness under the stated process.record policy acknowledgment
knowledge check
An assessment of particular knowledge at a stated point.administer a knowledge check
application question
A question testing use of a rule in a concrete situation.design an application question
scenario-based assessment
An assessment using realistic situations to test defined decisions.use scenario-based assessment
learning objective
A specific capability the instruction aims to develop.define learning objectives
role-based training
Instruction tailored to the tasks and risks of particular roles.deliver role-based training
job aid
A concise reference supporting a task at the point of work.provide a job aid
plain language
Wording that the intended audience can understand and use.explain in plain language
localization
Adaptation of material for a particular language, setting, or audience.review localization
accessibility
The ability of people with varied needs to access and use the material.check training accessibility
advice channel
A defined route for obtaining guidance on a question.identify the advice channel
speak-up culture
A work environment that supports raising questions and concerns.support a speak-up culture
knowledge gap
Missing or inaccurate understanding of specified information.identify knowledge gaps
application gap
Difficulty applying a known rule to a real situation.address application gaps
volunteer sample
A group whose members choose to participate.qualify a volunteer sample
representativeness
How well a group reflects the population relevant to a conclusion.assess representativeness
remedial learning
Targeted instruction addressing an identified learning need.provide remedial learning
follow-up assessment
A later check of a specified learning outcome.conduct a follow-up assessment
behavior measure
An indicator of what people actually do, distinct from attendance or reported knowledge.define behavior measures

W4 requires prior disclosure of outside paid work, including weekends. That does not mean every disclosed activity is prohibited. A training acknowledgment, a correct answer, and actual workplace behavior are different forms of evidence; each claim needs its own basis.

C Notice the language

Questions can indicate engagement, not defiance

People may stay silent if asking for clarification is treated as failure. Welcome questions about a concrete rule and provide accessible explanations. Keep completion reporting honest while testing whether employees can apply the policy in the situations their roles actually encounter.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the measure
Ninety-eight percent refers to recorded training completion.
State the denominator
Four hundred ninety of five hundred assigned employees completed it.
Keep the remainder visible
Ten assigned employees lack recorded completion.
Report the separate check
Eight of twenty volunteers answered the application question correctly.
Qualify the sample
We have not established that the volunteers represent the whole workforce.
Avoid the overclaim
We cannot report ninety-eight percent compliant behavior from this measure.
State the rule plainly
Under W4, disclose outside paid work before it begins.
Address the example
Weekend work is included in that disclosure requirement.
Separate disclosure and outcome
Disclosure does not automatically mean the work is prohibited.
Explain the next route
The designated reviewer applies the actual policy process.
Welcome the question
Asking how the rule applies is a useful part of learning.
Focus the instruction
Let us practice the distinction between disclosure timing and approval outcome.
Make help available
Give employees a clear advice channel for uncertain cases.
Check accessibility
Confirm that the wording and format are usable for the intended audience.
Test the specific objective
Use a follow-up scenario to check whether the timing rule is understood.
Close accurately
Report completion, the limited application result, and the targeted follow-up separately.
6 language and precision notes
Refers to
Defines the claim instead of letting a percentage imply broader success.
Lack recorded completion
Describes the system evidence without assuming a reason for the missing record.
Have not established
Makes the sample limitation explicit.
Before it begins
Places the required disclosure ahead of the activity.
Does not automatically
Separates a procedural requirement from its possible outcomes.
Separately
Prevents completion and effectiveness measures from being merged into a misleading headline.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which headline is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. Training completion is 98%; a separate volunteer check identified an application gap.

The evidence supports a completion measure and a limited separate check, not universal behavior or motive claims.

2. An employee plans paid weekend work. What does W4 require?

Answer and explanation

B. Disclosure before the work begins

The fictional rule covers weekend paid work and specifies prior disclosure, not an automatic prohibition.

3. What should be said about the 40% application result?

Answer and explanation

D. It describes eight correct answers among twenty volunteers, with representativeness unestablished.

A volunteer result has a defined scope and cannot automatically be generalized or treated as a causal finding.

4. Which follow-up is most relevant?

Answer and explanation

C. A clear W4 example, an advice route, and a later check of the same disclosure-timing distinction

The response targets the demonstrated application difficulty and supports questions rather than merely increasing an activity count.

  8. Ren

  10. Ren

Answers and explanations
  1. completion rate Turn 2. The completion rate is 490 of 500 assigned employees, not a verified rate of compliant workplace behavior.
  2. assigned cohort Turn 4. The assigned cohort defines the completion denominator, while missing records and approved exceptions need accurate status handling.
  3. volunteer sample Turn 6. The volunteer sample contains twenty self-selected participants and is not established as representative of all assigned employees.
  4. application gap Turn 8. The application gap concerns whether the prior-disclosure rule includes paid weekend work, rather than course attendance alone.
  5. acknowledgment Turn 10. An acknowledgment records the stated confirmation; it does not automatically prove understanding of every example or compliant conduct.
  6. job aid Turn 12. A job aid can support the task by stating the disclosure trigger, timing, and advice route without replacing the governing policy.
  7. role-based training Turn 14. Role-based training adapts examples to relevant tasks and risks while preserving the actual policy requirement.
  8. advice channel Turn 16. The advice channel gives employees a clear route for questions and should not treat a request for guidance as automatic misconduct.
  9. accessibility Turn 18. Accessibility includes usable wording and format for the audience; a recorded completion does not prove the material was readily understood.
  10. follow-up assessment Turn 20. The follow-up assessment checks the specific timing and outcome distinction rather than substituting another attendance count for understanding.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Completion does not demonstrate application

The lesson's training completion is 98%, but employees still ask how to apply the policy to common situations.

  1. Compliance training lead: Training completion is ninety-eight percent, but employees still ask how the policy applies to common situations.
  2. Business manager: Does that mean the completion figure is wrong, or that it measures something different from practical understanding?
  3. Compliance training lead: It measures completion. Applying the policy requires another kind of evidence, such as appropriate responses to relevant examples.
  4. Business manager: Then an attestation also shouldn't be described as proof that everyone can handle every situation correctly.
  5. Compliance training lead: Exactly. An attestation records what the person confirms under its wording; it doesn't answer every learning question.
  6. Business manager: Would role-based training help connect the general policy to the decisions employees actually face?
  7. Compliance training lead: It may help. We should identify the common questions and provide appropriate reinforcement through the approved guidance.
  8. Business manager: I'll avoid saying the culture is fully embedded because nearly everyone completed a module.
  9. Compliance training lead: Good. Keep participation, understanding, and actual behavior distinct in the training report.
  10. Business manager: We'll use the questions to plan reinforcement and assess practical understanding without discounting the completed training.
Acknowledging receipt is not certifying compliance

Separate fictional case: an internal attestation asks employees to acknowledge receiving a policy; a manager describes it as certification that every requirement has been met.

  1. Learning coordinator: This attestation asks people to acknowledge receiving the policy. It does not ask them to certify every requirement was met.
  2. Department manager: I described it as proof of compliance. That goes beyond the actual wording, then?
  3. Learning coordinator: Yes. Receipt, understanding, and demonstrated compliance are different claims, and this form establishes only the stated acknowledgment.
  4. Department manager: Could you explain that simply for the team without making the acknowledgment sound pointless?
  5. Learning coordinator: It records that the policy reached them. Other processes address questions about understanding and how work is performed.
  6. Department manager: Then we can value the record while keeping its evidential boundary clear.
  7. Learning coordinator: Exactly. Reinforcement can address practical questions, but it should not change the meaning of an existing attestation retrospectively.
  8. Department manager: I'll correct our summary and ask the training owner how to address any common application questions.
  9. Learning coordinator: Good. Use the actual policy and form wording rather than invent a broader assurance statement.
  10. Department manager: I'll distinguish acknowledgment from demonstrated application when I discuss our training status.
A general course and a role-specific decision

Separate fictional case: purchasing staff completed a general conflicts course but ask how to route a supplier-related disclosure; no individual conflict determination is discussed.

  1. Purchasing team manager: Our team completed the general conflicts course, but staff still ask where to route a supplier-related disclosure.
  2. Compliance educator: That suggests a practical application question. Course completion and knowing the role-specific next step are different things.
  3. Purchasing team manager: Should I interpret that question as proof that the training failed completely?
  4. Compliance educator: No. It identifies a reinforcement need, not a comprehensive finding about every part of the course.
  5. Purchasing team manager: Could we use a fictional purchasing example to explain the approved disclosure route without deciding a real case?
  6. Compliance educator: Yes. Role-based training connects the general expectation to the relevant action within the actual process.
  7. Purchasing team manager: Then we can ask staff to explain the next step, rather than repeat the course title.
  8. Compliance educator: Exactly. That checks understanding of the route, while any substantive conflict assessment remains with the authorized reviewer.
  9. Purchasing team manager: I'll bring the recurring questions and avoid treating an attestation as the answer to them.
  10. Compliance educator: I'll help plan the approved reinforcement and keep its learning purpose separate from a case determination.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Governance, Reporting, and Risk CommitteesAn approved plan is not a closed action+

Your goal

Report action status, remaining exposure, and requested decisions against explicit closure criteria.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

An approved plan is not a closed action

On 1 October, committee action G12 appears as complete because its remediation plan was approved on 12 September. Its due date was 30 September. The fictional closure rule requires implementation evidence and independent verification; neither is available. Action owner Owen has requested 15 October as a new due date, but no extension is approved. Risk coordinator Sara must correct the status for the 7 October committee meeting, preserve the history, and identify the decision needed. Approval of a revised date would not itself prove implementation or accept all remaining risk.

Owen: Remediation action owner / Sara: Risk committee coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why was G12 marked complete?

Answer and explanation

B. The plan was approved on 12 September.

The tracker confused plan approval with the separate closure requirements.

2. What evidence is needed for closure under the stated rule?

Answer and explanation

B. Implementation evidence and independent verification

The fictional closure rule requires both implementation support and independent verification.

3. What is the status of 15 October?

Answer and explanation

D. Requested but not approved

Owen has requested the new date, but no authorized extension is recorded.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

As of 1 October, the 30 September due date has passed and no approved extension exists. G12 therefore remains open and overdue under the supplied facts. The 15 October request must remain labeled proposed until the authorized process decides it.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
governance
The structures and processes for direction, oversight, and accountability.support effective governance
committee charter
A document defining a committee's purpose, scope, and authority.review the committee charter
terms of reference
The agreed purpose, responsibilities, and operating boundaries of a body or task.confirm terms of reference
quorum
The participation requirement for a body to conduct specified business under its rules.verify quorum
decision rights
The allocation of authority to make particular decisions.clarify decision rights
delegated authority
Decision power assigned within specified limits.verify delegated authority
action owner
The person responsible for delivering a defined action.identify the action owner
risk owner
The person accountable for managing a specified risk under the framework.identify the risk owner
closure criterion
A condition required to mark an action closed.apply closure criteria
implementation evidence
Records showing that the planned change has actually been put into effect.verify implementation evidence
independent verification
Assessment by an appropriately separate reviewer under the relevant requirements.obtain independent verification
residual exposure
The risk remaining after the relevant measures are assessed.describe residual exposure
proposed extension
A requested change to timing that has not yet been approved.record a proposed extension
approved extension
A timing change authorized through the relevant process.document an approved extension
overdue action
An action whose applicable due date has passed without completion.report overdue actions
status date
The date as of which a status report is accurate.state the status date
risk appetite
The broad amount or type of risk an organization is willing to take under its framework.define risk appetite
risk tolerance
Specified acceptable boundaries around relevant risk or performance measures.monitor risk tolerance
KRI
Key risk indicator; a defined measure used to monitor risk conditions.interpret a KRI
risk register
A record of identified risks, ownership, assessment, and responses.maintain the risk register
mitigation plan
Proposed actions intended to reduce a specified risk.approve a mitigation plan
decision log
A record of decisions, authority, reasons, and relevant conditions.maintain the decision log
exception reporting
Reporting departures from stated criteria or expectations.provide exception reporting
escalation threshold
A defined condition requiring referral to a higher or different authority.apply escalation thresholds

Approving a plan, changing a date, verifying implementation, and accepting risk are separate decisions. Do not report a percentage complete without a defined measurement basis. A later approved extension should preserve the original due date and decision history rather than erase the earlier status.

C Notice the language

Accurate bad news is usable management information

An overdue action can be uncomfortable to report, especially after a visible milestone has been achieved. Credit the approved plan while keeping the unfinished evidence visible. A clear status enables a real decision; a reassuring label can hide the very issue the committee needs to address.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the reporting date
This status is as of the first of October.
Credit the completed milestone
The remediation plan was approved on the twelfth of September.
Keep closure separate
Plan approval does not satisfy the closure criteria.
Name the missing evidence
Implementation evidence and independent verification are outstanding.
Correct the label
The action remains open and overdue against the thirtieth of September deadline.
Qualify the new date
The fifteenth of October is requested, not approved.
Preserve history
Keep the original deadline and the reason for each status change.
Ask for the actual decision
The committee needs to consider the extension request through its authority process.
Separate authority
A date change does not automatically accept the remaining risk.
Avoid an unsupported score
We have no defined basis for calling the action ninety percent complete.
Identify ownership
Owen owns delivery of the action; the risk owner addresses the remaining exposure.
State the impact
Explain what remains exposed while the action is unverified.
Define verification
The independent reviewer must assess the required implementation evidence.
Record conditions
Any decision should identify its scope, owner, conditions, and date.
Escalate appropriately
Use the actual escalation threshold and committee mandate.
Close the report
Show the achieved milestone, missing evidence, current deadline, and requested decision together.
6 language and precision notes
As of
Fixes the date to which the status applies.
Outstanding
Identifies unfinished evidence without pretending a plan is implementation.
Against
States which deadline is used to judge lateness.
Requested, not approved
Distinguishes a proposal from an authorized timing change.
Does not automatically
Prevents one decision from silently conferring broader authority.
Together
Keeps progress and unresolved exposure visible in the same report.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which 1 October status is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Open and overdue; plan approved, implementation and verification evidence outstanding.

The original deadline has passed and neither closure evidence nor an approved extension is available.

2. What would an approved extension establish by itself?

Answer and explanation

D. An authorized timing change under its stated conditions

A timing decision does not substitute for execution, evidence, verification, or separate risk authority.

3. Which record should be retained if timing changes?

Answer and explanation

C. The original deadline, extension decision, authority, reason, and new conditions

A traceable decision history explains both the original status and the authorized change.

4. What should the committee paper request?

Answer and explanation

B. The specific extension decision and any separate action needed for remaining exposure

A decision-ready paper identifies the actual request and keeps unresolved evidence and risk questions visible.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

Answers and explanations
  1. closure criteria Turn 2. The stated closure criteria require implementation evidence and independent verification, not merely an approved remediation plan.
  2. proposed extension Turn 4. The requested 15 October date remains proposed because no authorized extension decision has been recorded.
  3. status date Turn 6. The status date fixes the report at 1 October, when plan approval exists but the original deadline has passed without closure evidence.
  4. Implementation evidence Turn 8. Implementation evidence must show the change actually operating or deployed as required; an unsupported completion percentage cannot replace it.
  5. independent verification Turn 10. Independent verification requires the appropriately separate reviewer to assess the required evidence rather than accept the owner's completion label.
  6. residual exposure Turn 12. Residual exposure describes the risk still present while implementation and verification remain unresolved, not an assumed benefit from plan approval.
  7. decision rights Turn 14. Decision rights determine who may extend timing or accept risk; one approval does not automatically authorize the other.
  8. action owner Turn 16. The action owner delivers the defined work, while risk ownership and authorized acceptance may belong to different roles.
  9. decision log Turn 18. The decision log preserves the original deadline and the authorized change, including its reason, authority, date, and conditions.
  10. exception reporting Turn 20. Exception reporting makes the departure from the deadline and closure criteria visible so the committee can make the actual required decision.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Owen: G12 is marked complete in the tracker. The plan was approved on the twelfth of September, so I assumed we could close the action.
  2. Sara: The closure criteria require implementation evidence and independent verification. Neither is available yet. We can record the approved plan as a completed milestone without treating the whole action as closed.

  9. Owen: I can provide the deployment records when they are ready. Who then decides whether they meet the requirement?

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Plan approved, implementation evidence outstanding

The lesson's risk committee tracker marks an action complete because its plan was approved; implementation evidence remains outstanding.

  1. Risk committee secretary: The tracker marks remediation complete, but only the plan is approved. Implementation evidence is still outstanding.
  2. Action owner: Then the status should distinguish approval of the approach from completion of the actual work.
  3. Risk committee secretary: Correct. What can we report about implementation without guessing whether the missing evidence means nothing happened?
  4. Action owner: We can state that evidence is outstanding. I need to verify the work status before claiming either completion or nonperformance.
  5. Risk committee secretary: Good. The absence of a record here doesn't itself establish the entire operational history.
  6. Action owner: I'll obtain the relevant implementation status and evidence through our governance process.
  7. Risk committee secretary: Please keep any risk acceptance decision separate; approving a remediation plan doesn't automatically establish acceptance of remaining risk.
  8. Action owner: Understood. I won't infer authority or an acceptance decision from the plan approval.
  9. Risk committee secretary: The committee update will show the approved plan and open verification, with your follow-up responsibility identified.
  10. Action owner: I'll return with the verified status and any remaining gap before we reconsider completion.
Implementation complete, effectiveness testing pending

Separate fictional case: a remediation owner reports that a new check is implemented; the planned effectiveness test has not run and the closure criteria have not been reviewed in this conversation.

  1. Remediation owner: The new check has been implemented, but the planned effectiveness test has not yet run.
  2. Governance coordinator: Then we should report those stages separately. Have the actual closure criteria been checked before updating the risk committee tracker?
  3. Remediation owner: Not in this discussion. I'll consult the approved criteria rather than assume implementation alone permits closure.
  4. Governance coordinator: Good. Completed work deserves recognition, while the pending test and closure assessment must remain visible.
  5. Remediation owner: Would saying remediation progressing be enough, or is that too vague for the committee?
  6. Governance coordinator: Be specific: implemented, effectiveness testing pending, closure status subject to the applicable review.
  7. Remediation owner: I'll also identify who owns the test and confirm its timing rather than invent a completion date.
  8. Governance coordinator: Thank you. Keep the action status distinct from any formal risk acceptance or governance decision.
  9. Remediation owner: The update will name the completed implementation and the remaining verification dependency.
  10. Governance coordinator: That gives the committee a clear status without treating a plan, an action, and a tested result as equivalent.
A missing status update is not an accepted risk

Separate fictional case: a committee action has no recent owner update; its risk acceptance field is blank, and no acceptance decision is recorded.

  1. Governance analyst: The action has no recent owner update, and no risk acceptance decision is recorded in the tracker.
  2. Committee coordinator: Could we treat the silence as acceptance of the remaining risk and move the action out of the open list?
  3. Governance analyst: No. Missing communication doesn't establish an authorized decision. We need to confirm the actual status.
  4. Committee coordinator: Then our update should say status confirmation pending, not risk accepted or remediation complete.
  5. Governance analyst: Exactly. We also shouldn't infer that no work happened simply because the latest evidence isn't here.
  6. Committee coordinator: I'll ask the action owner for completed work, outstanding steps, and the supporting record.
  7. Governance analyst: Good. If a risk decision is needed, it must follow the actual governance route and authority.
  8. Committee coordinator: I'll keep that separate from the factual action update rather than create an acceptance entry myself.
  9. Governance analyst: Please bring back the confirmed information and identify any continuing gap for the committee.
  10. Committee coordinator: I will. The tracker will preserve the unresolved status until an evidence-based update or authorized decision is available.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An approved plan is not a closed action
  1. Read Owen and Sara aloud, then swap roles. Give the first-of-October status against the thirtieth-of-September deadline.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. Separate plan approval, implementation evidence, independent verification, and the proposed extension.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Report another action against its closure rule

Action K09 was due on 10 June. As of 12 June, its plan is approved but required implementation evidence and independent verification are missing. A 25 June extension has been requested but not approved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The status date is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 12 June

The report describes the position as of 12 June, after the original due date.

2. Owner: "The current action status is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. open and overdue

The due date has passed, closure evidence is missing, and no extension is approved.

3. Coordinator: "The twenty-fifth of June is a ___."

Answer and explanation

A. proposed extension

A requested date does not become the applicable deadline before authorization.

4. Owner: "Closure also requires ___."

Answer and explanation

C. independent verification

The stated closure rule requires verification as well as implementation evidence, not only plan approval.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Course: Legal Operations and Compliance English
